<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2021 Mar 9; Revised 2021 Sep 6; Collection date 2021.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2021 The Authors</div>
<p>This is an open access article under the CC BY license (http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>

</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC8605257  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34673282/" class="usa-link">34673282</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abs0010"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Helminths are masters at manipulating host's immune response. Especially, parasitic nematodes have evolved strategies that allow them to evade, suppress, or modulate host's immune response to persist and spread in the host's organism. While the immunomodulatory effects of nematodes on their hosts are studied with a great commitment, very little is known about nematodes' own immune system, immune response to their pathogens, and interactions between parasites and bacteria in the host's organism. To illustrate the response of the parasitic nematode <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. during simulated interaction with <em>Escherichia coli</em>, different concentrations of lipopolysaccharide (LPS) were used, and the proteomic analysis with isobaric mass tags for relative and absolute quantification (tandem mass tag–based LC–MS/MS) was performed. In addition, gene expression and biochemical analyses of selected markers of oxidative stress were determined. The results revealed 1148 proteins in a group of which 115 were identified as differentially regulated proteins, for example, peroxiredoxin, thioredoxin, and macrophage migration inhibitory factor. Gene Ontology annotation and Reactome pathway analysis indicated that metabolic pathways related to catalytic activity, oxidation–reduction processes, antioxidant activity, response to stress, and innate immune system were the most common, in which differentially regulated proteins were involved. Further biochemical analyses let us confirm that the LPS induced the oxidative stress response, which plays a key role in the innate immunity of parasitic nematodes. Our findings, to our knowledge, indicate for the first time, the complexity of the interaction of parasitic nematode, <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. with bacterial LPS, which mimics the coexistence of helminth and gut bacteria in the host. The simulation of this crosstalk led us to conclude that the obtained results could be hugely valuable in the integrated systems biology approach to describe a relationship between parasite, host, and its commensal bacteria.</p>
<section id="kwrds0010" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Anisakis simplex</em>, lipopolysaccharide, parasite–bacteria interrelationship, oxidative stress, LC–MS/MS</p></section><section id="kwrds0015" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Abbreviations:</strong> ABTS, 2,2'-azinobis-(3-ethylbenzothiazoline-6-sulfonic acid); BP, biological process; cDNA, complementary DNA; DRP, differentially regulated protein; ERAP1, endoplasmic reticulum aminopeptidase 1; FC, fold change; <em>gdf 11</em>, growth/differentiation factor 11; GO, Gene Ontology; GST, glutathione-<em>S</em>-transferase; HSD, honest significant difference; IL, interleukin; LPS, lipopolysaccharide; MF, molecular function; MHC, major histocompatibility complex; MIF, macrophage migration inhibitory factor; PRDX, peroxiredoxin; ROS, reactive oxygen species; RQ, relative quantification; TGF-β, transforming growth factor β; TMT, tandem mass tag</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="abs0015"><h2>Graphical Abstract</h2>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="undfig1"><p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/012cbfa9986b/fx1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="664" width="664" alt="graphic file with name fx1.jpg"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/undfig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section class="abstract" id="abs0020"><h2>Highlights</h2>
<ul id="ulist0010" class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li id="u0010">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0010">LPS induces a complex proteomic response in a parasitic nematode <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0015">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0015">LPS induces oxidative stress in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0020">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0020">Peroxiredoxins are strongly involved in the response to LPS in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s.</p>
</li>
<li id="u0025">
<span class="label">•</span><p class="display-inline" id="p0025">This experimental setup mimics the coexistence of parasite and bacteria in the host.</p>
</li>
</ul></section><section class="abstract" id="abs0025"><h2>In Brief</h2>
<p><em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. is a seafood-borne parasite that is involved in human infections. This experimental setup mimics the coexistence of helminth and gut bacteria in the host. Describing the mechanisms of the proteomic response of <em>A. simplex</em> to LPS can contribute to better understanding the parasite biology and more effective treatment of anisakiasis. Our findings indicate the complexity of the proteomic response of this parasite to LPS. Obtained results are valuable in planning future strategies for studying helminths.</p></section><hr class="headless">
<p id="p0030"><em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. is one of the seafood-borne parasitic nematodes, which is commonly involved in human infections (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">1</a>). The disease caused by the Anisakis genus is called anisakiasis (<a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2">2</a>, <a href="#bib3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib3">3</a>). Humans, as accidental hosts, can be infected by third-stage larvae (L3) present in raw or undercooked fish or cephalopods (<a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2">2</a>). The infection with <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. can cause gastrointestinal symptoms or mild to severe allergic reactions (<a href="#bib4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib4">4</a>, <a href="#bib5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib5">5</a>). During recent years, progress in the food industry and its globalization have changed the eating habits of people all over the world (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">1</a>). According to the European Food Safety Authority, nematode infections from food are ubiquitous in Europe. In addition, an expert panel has classified <em>A. simplex</em> as a biohazardous organism (<a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6">6</a>, <a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7">7</a>). Moreover, climate change and increasing water temperatures have caused <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. to expand its range, allowing this species to occur in seas and oceans, where it was not previously found (<a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8">8</a>). Anisakiasis is a relatively new and rapidly spreading zoonosis that poses a significant threat to humans and animals. The disease causes economic losses in the fisheries sector by undermining consumer confidence and reducing demand for potentially infected fish. At a time when travel is widespread and international trade is rapidly increasing, anisakiasis is becoming a global problem. The growing popularity of exotic dishes prepared from raw fish and cephalopods and the widespread practice of undercooking seafood are also contributing to the spread of the disease (<a href="#bib9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib9">9</a>, <a href="#bib10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib10">10</a>, <a href="#bib11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib11">11</a>, <a href="#bib12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib12">12</a>). All these could be a reason for the increasing number of anisakiasis cases. The incidence of anisakiasis continues to increase, and 20,000 new cases were reported in 2010. According to the quantitative risk assessment model, the prevalence of anisakiasis in Europe will increase to 7500 to 8500 cases per year (<a href="#bib13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib13">13</a>).</p>
<p id="p0035">Recent studies reported that the presence of helminth infection alters the composition of the bacterial intestinal microbiota and, conversely, that the presence and composition of the bacterial microbiota affect helminth colonization and persistence within mammalian hosts (<a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">14</a>). The crosstalk between helminths and the bacteria of the host and their interactions are largely unknown. Currently, the concept of parasite–bacteria interactions in vertebrate bodies is of great interest. These interactions can be neutral, harmful, or have beneficial effects (<a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15">15</a>, <a href="#bib16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib16">16</a>). In general, intestinal helminths increase the expression of antimicrobial peptides in the digestive tract, such as angiogenin 4 after infection with <em>Trichuris muris</em> and C-type lectin RegIIIγ following <em>Heligmosomoides polygyrus</em> infection in mice (<a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17">17</a>, <a href="#bib18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib18">18</a>). This kind of interaction can lead to changes in the composition of the microbiota after helminth infetion. On the other hand, the bacterial microbiota-specific immune response during parasite infection may reduce the immune response to helminth antigens. Alternatively, the microbiota can increase mucosal or systemic immunity to parasitic infections by changing innate or adaptive immunity (<a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19">19</a>).</p>
<p id="p0040">Compared with another species, relatively little is known about the immune responses in the <em>A. simplex</em> nematode. In common with other invertebrates, nematodes defense against pathogens that rely on innate immune response (<a href="#bib20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib20">20</a>). During defense processes, the immune system triggers the formation of large amounts of reactive oxygen species (ROS) like superoxide anion (O<sub>2</sub>), hydroxyl radical (OH), and hydrogen peroxide (H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>), which are accumulated in the cells (<a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">21</a>). ROS act as signaling molecules and represent an efficient and highly conserved effector mechanism to eliminate pathogens in animals and plants (<a href="#bib22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib22">22</a>). In a free-living nematode, <em>Caenorhabditis elegans</em>, ROS can activate protective cellular mechanisms to promote longevity, pathogen defense responses, and wound healing (<a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23">23</a>). Moreover, <em>C. elegans</em> infected with <em>Enterococcus faecalis</em> produce ROS <em>via</em> the dual oxidase Duox1/BLI-3 (present in the intestine), which represents a protective antimicrobial response (<a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24">24</a>, <a href="#bib25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib25">25</a>). Nematodes can be also exposed to ROS released from immune effector cells like macrophages, neutrophils, and eosinophils produced within their vertebrate hosts (<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">26</a>). Despite the fact that ROS plays a crucial role in defense against bacterial infection, their overexpression or production by the parasite hosts' require mechanisms by which nematodes can protect themselves (<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">26</a>). Therefore, parasitic nematodes have developed effective antioxidant defense systems, including enzymes that deal with the ROS, like superoxide dismutase, catalase, and peroxiredoxins (PRDXs), to detoxify and regulate intracellular homeostasis (<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">26</a>).</p>
<p id="p0045">Although many of those mechanisms have been described on intestinal nematodes when considering them as pathogenic to their hosts (mammalian host–parasite relationship), the global overview on the molecular processes occurring in the helminths during the coexistence with bacteria and their influence on parasites have not been fully characterized (parasite–bacteria interrelationship). The shared effects of bacteria and helminths include suppression of the host immunity to permit their survival. Therefore, they have common strategies that include the activation of regulatory T cells by some bacteria such as <em>Bacteroides fragilis</em> and <em>Lactobacillus</em> spp. and by some parasites including <em>H. polygyrus</em> and <em>Strongyloides ratti</em> (<a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">14</a>). The mammalian immune system has learned to distinguish which microorganisms reject or accept. Moreover, both commensal bacteria and intestinal helminths have developed similar strategies of modulating host immunity. Interestingly, they have developed a surprising degree of dialog with a common goal of establishing new homeostasis in the host intestinal tract to survive (<a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14">14</a>, <a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15">15</a>, <a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27">27</a>). Therefore, it is not known whether the digestive tract bacteria should be treated as pathogenic to intestinal parasites or as coexisting organisms whose metabolic pathways are to some extent specifically integrated (<a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">28</a>).</p>
<p id="p0050">To study the interaction of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. with the human microbiome, we decided to treat the larvae with the lipopolysaccharide (LPS) of <em>Escherichia coli</em>, a bacterium found in the human gut. The results by Guardone <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib29">29</a>) showed that gastric and intestinal localizations of Anisakis larvae during infection had very similar frequencies, with only a slightly higher frequency of gastric lesions. Also, in a retrospective case series study conducted in Tokyo, Japan, 47% of patients had gastric anisakiasis and 53% had small intestinal anisakiasis (<a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30">30</a>). However, other authors note that in Japan the acute gastric form predominates (95%), whereas in Europe, the chronic intestinal form seems to be more common (<a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31">31</a>, <a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32">32</a>). For all these reasons, we decided to try to explain possible interactions of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. with a representative of the human gut microbiome, namely <em>E. coli</em>, using a rapidly developing branch of biology, proteomics.</p>
<p id="p0055">In the last 2 decades, proteomics has become a powerful tool for describing dynamically changing biological systems (<a href="#bib33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib33">33</a>). Proteomics methods were not only used for the identification and quantification of the protein composition of cells, tissue sections, and whole organisms at a certain time, collectively termed “the proteome” but also to describe protein modifications and interactions (<a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34">34</a>). Proteomics in the studies on parasitic species has been widely used (<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1">1</a>, <a href="#bib35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib35">35</a>). Moreover, proteomics on <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. was previously performed (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">36</a>, <a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">37</a>) but never in the context of parasite–bacteria interactions.</p>
<p id="p0060">Accordingly, in this work, using the tandem mass tag (TMT)-based quantitative proteomics method based on TMT-isobaric mass tag labeling and LC–MS/MS analysis in an LTQ-Orbitrap Elite mass spectrometer, we present for the first time the proteome profiling of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae stimulated by bacterial LPS from <em>E. coli</em>, an <em>in vitro</em> model of the interplay between parasites and bacteria.</p>
<section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Experimental Procedures</h2>
<section id="sec1.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Anisakis simplex</h3>
<p id="p0065">All experiments were performed on the alive L3 larvae of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. from Baltic herring (<em>Clupea harengus membras</em>) caught in the coastal waters of the southern Baltic Sea. All impurities were removed from the harvested larvae. Then, the larvae were washed several times in a sterile 0.9% NaCl and stored at 4 °C until further analyses. At the beginning of the study, five of the larvae were subjected to the taxonomic identification by Anis Sensitive Sniper Real-Time PCR kit (A &amp; A Biotechnology, Gdynia, Poland) as described before (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">36</a>).</p></section><section id="sec1.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Preliminary Study</h3>
<section id="sec1.2.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In Vitro</em> Culture With LPS</h4>
<p id="p0070">To choose the optimal LPS concentrations for an <em>in vitro</em> model of parasite–bacteria interaction, the preliminary culture of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae was conducted under anerobic conditions (37 °C, 5% CO<sub>2</sub>) in the presence of LPS isolated from <em>E. coli</em> (Sigma–Aldrich; L8274-10MG) at different concentrations: 0.05, 0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8, 1, and 2 μg/ml for 12 h. The procedure of <em>in vitro</em> culture was carried out as described previously by Iglesias <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib38">38</a>) using the reagents as described before (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">36</a>, <a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>). Three <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae were placed in each well of the 6-well culture plates (BD Biosciences) (27 in total). The parasites without LPS were cultured as a control (three larvae × three wells; nine in total).</p></section><section id="sec1.2.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Real-Time PCR Analysis of Growth/Differentiation Factor 11</h4>
<p id="p0075">It was reported before that homologs of human transforming growth factor β (TGF-β) from an intestinal helminths mimics host's TGF-β biological and functional properties and induces potently suppressive regulatory T cells engaged in inflammatory process (<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40">40</a>). In <em>A. simplex</em> s.s., such homolog of human TGF-β, that is, growth/differentiation factor 11 (<em>gdf 11</em>), was previously identified by Łopieńska-Biernat <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41">41</a>) during genome-wide analysis of this species. To determine the <em>gdf 11</em> mRNA level in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s., the real-time PCR was performed. The primers for the <em>gdf 11</em> were designed using the Primer3Plus software (<a href="#bib42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib42">42</a>) (ELIXIR) based on the sequence deposited in GenBank (<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nuccore/MF069104.1" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">MF069104.1</a>) and listed in <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Table S1</a>. The actin was used as the endogenous reference gene (<a href="#bib43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib43">43</a>). The reaction was performed as described before (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">36</a>). In brief, the total RNA of the larvae was isolated using a Total RNA Mini Kit (A &amp; A Biotechnology), and complementary DNA (cDNA) was obtained using TranScriba Kit (A &amp; A Biotechnology) according to the manufacturer's protocols. The real-time PCR mixture contained 1 μl of cDNA, 5 μl of 2× SYBR RT PCR MIX SYBR B (A &amp; A Biotechnology), 0.25 μl of each primer, 0.25 μl of Rox Reference Dye II (A &amp; A Biotechnology), and 3.25 μl of nuclease-free water to a final volume of 10 μl. The reactions were performed in six replicates on a 7500 Fast Real-Time PCR thermocycler (Applied Biosystems). The relative expression, presented as the fold change (FC) relative to the untreated control, as well as normalized to an endogenous reference gene (actin) (relative quantification [RQ] = 1), was calculated using the comparative Pfaffl method (<a href="#bib44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib44">44</a>). The data were expressed as means ± SDs. Statistical analysis was performed using one-way ANOVA in Prism 8 software (GraphPad Software, Inc). Differences between means were assessed by Tukey's multiple comparison test. <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant: 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗).</p></section></section><section id="sec1.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In Vitro</em> Culture of <em>A. simplex</em> With LPS</h3>
<p id="p0080">Based on the results of the preliminary study (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>), for further experiments, two concentrations of LPS (0.2 μg/ml—the lowest expression of <em>gdf 11</em> and 1 μg/ml—the highest expression of <em>gdf 11</em>) were selected to induce the proteomic response in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. The <em>in vitro</em> culture of the <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae with LPS was performed as described in “<a href="#sec1.2.1" class="usa-link">In Vitro Culture With LPS</a>” section. About 20 larvae were placed in each well of the culture plate (BD Biosciences) and incubated for 12 h with LPS (two concentrations × 20 larvae × three replicates; 120 larvae in total). The parasites without LPS were cultured as a control (20 larvae × three replicates).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/8c651b78f045/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="657" width="667" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>The expression of <em>gdf 11</em> in <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae exposed <em>in vitro</em> (12 h) to different LPS concentrations (0.05, 0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8, 1, and 2 μg/ml).</strong> Depicted values indicate means of six replicates ± SD. The data were presented as the fold change in gene expression normalized to an endogenous reference gene and relative to the untreated control (relative quantification [RQ] = 1). <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant, where 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗). LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec1.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Protein Extraction, Preparation, and Analyses</h3>
<section id="sec1.4.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Protein Extraction</h4>
<p id="p0085">Proteins were extracted as described before by Stryiński <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>). After <em>in vitro</em> culture, the parasites (ten larvae × three replicates of each sample: 0.2 μg/ml abbreviated further as “LPS 0.2” and 1 μg/ml abbreviated further as “LPS 1” and control) were crushed manually with a sterile plastic pestle in 2 ml centrifuge tubes. Then, protein extraction was performed in 1.5 ml of lysis buffer (60 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 1% lauryl maltoside, 5 mM PMSF, and 1% DTT). The protein concentration was quantified using the bicinchoninic acid method (Pierce BCA Protein Assay Kit; Thermo Fisher Scientific) according to the manufacturer's protocol. Then, the SDS-PAGE electrophoresis was performed as a control step to determine whether the protein extraction was done correctly (<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>). Running conditions were 80 V for the first 20 min and then 120 V until the end of the electrophoresis. Gels were silver stained using the Pierce Silver Stain for Mass Spectrometry kit (Thermo Fisher Scientific) according to the manufacturer's protocol (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Fig. S1</a><em>A</em>). A total of 100 μg of the protein from each sample (nine samples in total) was transferred into new tubes, and methanol/chloroform precipitation was performed as described by Carrera <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib45">45</a>). Then, ultrafast tryptic digestion with the simultaneous application of high-intensity focused ultrasound was carried out, as described previously by Stryiński <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">37</a>, <a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>).</p></section><section id="sec1.4.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">TMT Labeling and Reversed-Phase Fractionation</h4>
<p id="p0090">The TMT 10-plex isobaric label reagents (0.8 mg; Thermo Fisher Scientific) were resuspended in 41 μl of anhydrous acetonitrile and added to 100 μg of protein digest, as described by Stryiński <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>). Within the experiment, samples were labeled with TMT10-plex in triplicate (LPS 0.2: 128N, 128C, and 129N; LPS 1: 129C, 130N, and 130C; without LPS as a control: 126, 127N, and 127C). Samples were combined in a new tube at equal amounts according to the manufacturer's instructions. The TMT-labeled peptide concentration was measured using a Pierce Quantitative Colorimetric Peptide Assay (Thermo Fisher Scientific) according to the manufacturer's instructions. To increase the number of peptide identifications, eliminate the interference from coisolated ions and achieve results comparable to the MS3-based methods (<a href="#bib46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib46">46</a>), the combined sample was fractionated using a Pierce High-pH Reversed-Phase Peptide Fractionation Kit (Thermo Fisher Scientific) following the manufacturer's instructions (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Figs. S1</a><em>C</em> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S2–S4</a>). The peptide concentration in each fraction was determined by colorimetric analysis using the Quantitative Colorimetric Peptide Assay (Thermo Fisher Scientific) following the manufacturer's instructions. Then, fractions were evaporated to dryness using vacuum centrifugation (SpeedVac concentrator; Thermo Fisher Scientific). The samples (eight fractions plus the wash and flow throughput) were stored at −80 °C until further analysis.</p></section><section id="sec1.4.3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">LC–MS/MS Analysis and Data Processing</h4>
<p id="p0095">Peptide fractions were acidified with 0.1% formic acid, cleaned on a C<sub>18</sub> MicroSpin column (The Nest Group), and analyzed by LC–MS/MS using a Proxeon EASY-nLC II liquid chromatography system (Thermo Fisher Scientific) coupled to an LTQ-Orbitrap Elite mass spectrometer (Thermo Fisher Scientific). Peptide separation (1 μg) was done as described by Stryiński <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">37</a>, <a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39">39</a>).</p>
<p id="p0100">All acquired MS/MS spectra were analyzed using SEQUEST-HT (Proteome Discoverer 2.4 package; Thermo Fisher Scientific) against a custom-made database containing protein entries for <em>A. simplex</em> plus “Nematoda,” available in the UniProt/TrEMBL database (downloaded November 2019; 1,847,926 entries). The following restrictions were used: full tryptic cleavage with up to two missed cleavage sites and tolerances of 10 ppm for parent ions and 0.06 Da for MS/MS fragment ions. TMT labeling (+229.163 Da on N termini and lysine residues) and carbamidomethylation of cysteine (+57.021 Da) were set as fixed modifications. The permissible variable modifications were methionine oxidation (+15.994 Da), acetylation (+42.011 Da) of the N terminus of the protein, and deamidation (+0.984 Da) of asparagine and glutamine. Moreover, searching parameters included four maximal dynamic modification sites (<a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">37</a>).</p></section><section id="sec1.4.4"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical Analysis</h4>
<p id="p0105">The results were subjected to statistical analysis to determine the peptide false discovery rate using a decoy database and the Target Decoy PSM Validator algorithm (<a href="#bib47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib47">47</a>). The false discovery rate was kept below 1%, and for further analysis, only proteins meeting selected criteria were submitted: (a) proteins quantified with at least two unique peptides (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Fig. S1</a><em>B</em>), (b) proteins with different protein IDs, (c) proteins matched for organisms from Rhabditida order, and (d) proteins classified as characterized. RQ was performed using the Quantification Mode and normalization against total peptide amount (Proteome Discoverer 2.4 package).</p>
<p id="p0110">After RQ, several filters were applied to obtain the final list of differentially regulated proteins (DRPs): (a) at least a one FC in normalized ratios of control <em>versus</em> LPS 0.2, control <em>versus</em> LPS 1, and LPS 1 <em>versus</em> LPS 0.2 and (b) ANOVA on ranks and Tukey honest significant difference (HSD) post hoc test (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05).</p></section><section id="sec1.4.5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Functional Categories of Identified Proteins</h4>
<p id="p0115">The final list of proteins obtained after RQ (1148) was classified into three different categories of Gene Ontology (GO): biological processes (BPs), cell components, and molecular functions (MFs). GO analysis was performed using g:GOSt, the core of the g:Profiler (ELIXIR) that performs statistical enrichment analysis (<a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48">48</a>) (<a href="https://biit.cs.ut.ee/gprofiler/gost" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://biit.cs.ut.ee/gprofiler/gost</a>). The g:GOSt web-based tool applied an overrepresentation test controlled with the g:SCS algorithm. The significantly enriched functional GO categories were reported by comparing the input data with the background of GO annotations for parasite-specific data from WormBase ParaSite (<em>A. simplex</em> PRJEB496).</p></section><section id="sec1.4.6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Network Analysis</h4>
<p id="p0120">Network analysis was performed by submitting the DRP dataset to Cytoscape (version 3.8.0.; NIGMS), a software platform for visualizing complex networks, and analyzed by stringApp (version 1.5.1.) (<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">49</a>). Interactions have been identified by comparing the input data with the background of the <em>C. elegans</em>, the phylogenetically closest nematode available in the stringApp database. The network was limited only to the proteins that have at least one interaction with other proteins submitted to the analysis. The analyzed pathways were classified with the use of Reactome biological pathways database (<a href="#bib50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib50">50</a>) based on <em>C. elegans</em> classification.</p></section></section><section id="sec1.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Biochemical Analyses</h3>
<section id="sec1.5.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Extract Preparation for Biochemical Analyzes</h4>
<p id="p0125">The extract of the larvae after <em>in vitro</em> culture (ten larvae × three replicates of each sample: LPS 0.2, LPS 1, and control) for biochemical analyses was prepared by mechanical homogenization (Omni tissue Homogenizer, Omni, Inc) in sterile PBS (pH = 7.4). The extracts were centrifugated in 4 °C by 15 min (5000<em>g</em>), and supernatants were transferred into new tubes in amount of 300 μl. The protein concentration was determined using the bicinchoninic acid method (Pierce BCA Protein Assay Kit; Thermo Fisher Scientific) according to the manufacturer's protocol.</p></section><section id="sec1.5.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">PRDX Activity Assay</h4>
<p id="p0130">Reduction of peroxide (H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>) was measured using the ferrous oxidation–xylenol orange assay (Pierce quantitative peroxide assay kit; Thermo Fisher Scientific). Reactions were performed at 22 °C and initiated by mixing 20 μl of previously prepared supernatants with 200 μl of working reagent. The control reactions were run in parallel using supernatants from control samples. After incubation for 30 min, absorbance was measured at 562 nm on a microplate reader (Asys UVM340; Biochrom). Peroxide standards (4–1000 μM) were included in each assay and used to calculate the quantities of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. Three technical replicates out of each biological replicate (3 × LPS 0.2, 3 × LPS 1, and 3 × control) were performed. PRDX activity was described based on quantities of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, where the lower quantity of remaining H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> indicates the higher activity of PRDXs compared with the control.</p></section><section id="sec1.5.3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Antioxidant Capacity</h4>
<p id="p0135">Total antioxidant capacity was analyzed by the improved 2,2'-azinobis-(3-ethylbenzothiazoline-6-sulfonic acid) (ABTS) radical cation decolorization assay according to Re <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51">51</a>). The preformed radical monocation of ABTS∗+ is generated by oxidation of ABTS with potassium persulfate and is reduced in the presence of such hydrogen-donating antioxidants. Three technical replicates out of each biological replicate (3 × LPS 0.2, 3 × LPS 1, and 3 × control) were performed. The results were calculated as Trolox (a water-soluble analog of vitamin E) equivalents per liter.</p></section><section id="sec1.5.4"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">GSH Content</h4>
<p id="p0140">GSH content was measured according to the Ellman method (<a href="#bib52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib52">52</a>), which was involved in nonenzymatic reduction of 5,5′-dithiobis (2-nitrobenzoic acid), by GSH. The 50 μl of previously prepared larvae supernatants were deproteinized with 10% trichloroacetic acid at the 1:12 ratio before analysis. Three technical replicates out of each biological replicate (3 × LPS 0.2, 3 × LPS 1, and 3 × control) were performed. GSH content was calculated based on the standard curve plotted for serial dilution of 10 mM GSH solution.</p></section><section id="sec1.5.5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Glutathione <em>S</em>-Transferase Activity</h4>
<p id="p0145">The glutathione-<em>S</em>-transferase (GST) activity was determined using the Rice-Evans method (<a href="#bib53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib53">53</a>). Enzyme activity was calculated based on the millimolar absorption coefficient (9.6 mmol<sup>−1</sup>/cm<sup>−1</sup>) for GSH conjugate formed from 1-chloro-2,4-dinitrobenzene. Three technical replicates out of each biological replicate (3 × LPS 0.2, 3 × LPS 1, and 3 × control) were performed. The GST activity was converted into the arbitrary units per 1 mg of protein.</p></section><section id="sec1.5.6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical Analysis for Biochemical Analyses</h4>
<p id="p0150">Statistical analyses for the obtained results were performed using ordinary one-way ANOVA (with Tukey post hoc test) in Prism 8 software (GraphPad Software, Inc). <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant, where 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗).</p></section></section><section id="sec1.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Real-Time PCR Analysis</h3>
<p id="p0155">The proteins involved in the oxidative stress, classified after LC–MS/MS analysis, were chosen to determine the mRNA expression in the L3 larvae of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. by real-time PCR. The <em>prdx-1</em> gene was selected, based on its stable protein abundance (not modulated). The <em>thioredoxin domain–containing protein</em> and <em>thioredoxin domain–containing protein 12</em> as well as <em>prdx-3</em> genes were selected based on the downregulation of its proteins caused by LPS, whereas <em>prdx-2</em> was selected because of the upregulation of PRDX-2 protein in one of LPS-treated <em>versus</em> untreated control. The primers for the selected five genes were designed using the Primer3Plus software (<a href="#bib42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib42">42</a>) (ELIXIR) and listed in <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Table S1</a>. The protocol to perform real-time PCR and statistical analysis was described in “<a href="#sec1.2.2" class="usa-link">Real-Time PCR Analysis of Growth/Differentiation Factor 11</a>” section. The cDNA to perform the reaction was obtained during the preliminary study.</p></section><section id="sec1.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Experimental Design and Statistical Rationale</h3>
<p id="p0160">To test the three different conditions (control, and two LPS concentrations: 0.2 μg/ml abbreviated as “LPS 0.2” and 1 μg/ml abbreviated as “LPS 1”), three biological replicates of each <em>in vitro</em> culture (condition) of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. were performed (nine in total). About 20 larvae were placed in each well of the culture plate (three conditions × 20 larvae × three biological replicates; 180 larvae in total).</p>
<p id="p0165">After <em>in vitro</em> incubation, each sample for LC–MS/MS analysis was prepared out of ten larvae (× three conditions × three biological replicates). Within the experiment, samples were labeled with TMT10-plex in triplicate (LPS 0.2: 128N, 128C, 129N; LPS 1: 129C, 130N, and 130C; and without LPS as a control: 126, 127N, and 127C). The Kruskal–Wallis one-way ANOVA on ranks and Tukey HSD post hoc test (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) was performed to identify proteins with significant higher or lower abundance.</p>
<p id="p0170">The second part of the larvae from each <em>in vitro</em> culture (three conditions × ten larvae × three biological replicates) was used to prepare extract of the larvae for the biochemical analyses. For each biochemical analysis, three technical replicates out of each biological replicate (3 × LPS 0.2, 3 × LPS 1, and 3 × control) were performed. The data were expressed as means ± SDs. Statistical analysis was performed using one-way ANOVA. Differences between means were assessed by Tukey's multiple comparison test. <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant, where 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗).</p></section></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="sec2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Preliminary Study to Determine the Doses of LPS</h3>
<p id="p0175">The expression of the <em>gdf 11</em> in L3 larvae of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. varied in the presence of different doses of LPS (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>). The LPS doses—0.2 and 1 μg/ml—were selected, based on their significant impact on the lowest and highest expression of <em>gdf 11</em>, respectively, when compared with the control.</p></section><section id="sec2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Specific Proteome Changes in <em>Anisakis simples</em> s.s. After LPS Treatment</h3>
<p id="p0180">As a result of the LC–MS/MS analysis, we identified in a total 4222 master proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S1</a>). These data were further processed according to the selected criteria: (a) proteins quantified with at least two unique peptides (2157 proteins left), (b) proteins with different protein IDs (1744 proteins left), (c) proteins matched only for organisms from Rhabditida order (1728 proteins left), and (d) characterized proteins (1148 proteins left). For further analysis, we used in total 1148 proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S2</a>).</p>
<p id="p0185">After RQ, next several filters were applied to obtain the final list of DRPs: (a) at least a one FC in normalized ratios of control <em>versus</em> LPS 0.2, control <em>versus</em> LPS 1, and LPS 1 <em>versus</em> LPS 0.2; (b) ANOVA and Tukey HSD post hoc test (<em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S2</a>). Volcano plot representations of DRPs are shown in <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>, <em>A</em>–<em>C</em>. In all presented volcano plots, the most upregulated proteins were toward the right (<em>green</em>), the most downregulated proteins were toward the left (<em>red</em>), and out of them, the most statistically significant proteins were toward the top.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/119d3afbb18c/gr2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1391" width="702" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>Detailed visualization of differentially regulated proteins of L3 larvae of <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. proteome during LPS-induced inflammation.</strong> Volcano-plot representations of statistical analysis of performed comparisons: (<em>A</em>) 0.2 μg/ml of LPS <em>versus</em> control, (<em>B</em>) 1 μg/ml of LPS <em>versus</em> control, and (<em>C</em>) 1 μg/ml of LPS <em>versus</em> 0.2 μg/ml of LPS. The most upregulated proteins were toward the right (<em>green</em>), the most downregulated proteins were toward the left (<em>red</em>), and out of them, the most statistically significant proteins were toward the top. The legend for protein IDs is presented in <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S2</a>. LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0190">The response of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. to the LPS-induced inflammation was different, depending on the dose of LPS. Proteomic analysis indicated that 115 proteins were differentially regulated in the LPS-treated larvae compared with the control, of which 54 were upregulated and 45 were downregulated in the larvae cultured with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS, whereas 11 proteins were upregulated and 19 were downregulated in the larvae cultured with 1 μg/ml of LPS (FC = 1.0; <em>p</em> ≤ 0.05) (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, <em>A</em>–<em>C</em> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>).</p>
<p id="p0195">In the group of upregulated proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>), we identified those with catalytic activity including oxidoreductases (<em>i.e.</em>, dihydroorotate dehydrogenase, inosine-5′-monophosphate dehydrogenase, pyruvate dehydrogenase E1 component subunit alpha, or glutamate dehydrogenase) and hydrolases (<em>i.e.</em>, Tr-type G domain–containing protein, 1,4-alpha-glucan branching enzyme, or MPN domain–containing protein). Among the proteins regulating antioxidant response, we found PRDX 2, thioredoxin GSH reductase, and 60S ribosomal protein L31. In the group of downregulated proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>), we identified other antioxidant proteins such as PRDX 3 and two thioredoxin domain–containing proteins. In the cohort of DRPs, we also noticed proteins that take part in immune response, for example, cathepsin D, galectin, macrophage migration inhibitor factor, twitchin, or tetraspanin. Additional graphical representation of differences in the abundance of DRPs between the control and LPS-treated larvae were shown in <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/431c410b0b9e/gr3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="2084" width="614" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>Visualization of the DRPs statistically significant (adjusted <em>p</em> &lt; 0.05) in two comparisons (control <em>versus</em> LPS 0.2, control <em>versus</em> LPS 1).</strong><em>Red</em> (upregulated) and <em>green</em> (downregulated) <em>squares</em> describe increased and decreased expression in the compared groups, respectively. DRP, differentially regulated protein; LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">DRPs Are Associated With Diverse Biological Pathways</h3>
<p id="p0200">The 1148 proteins were assigned to functional ontology annotations. GO analysis divided the input proteins into three different categories: MF (21 different functions), BPs (55 different processes), and cellular components (51 different components) (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>; <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S3</a>). The top ten subcategories (a = 0.05) assigned for each of three main GO annotations are presented in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>. The functions assigned to the MF category, with predominant activity, were <em>structural constituent of ribosome</em> and <em>structural molecule activity</em> (GO: 000373548 proteins and GO: 000519862 proteins), <em>translation factor activity and regulator activity</em> (GO: 000813519 proteins and GO: 009007919 proteins), and <em>oxidoreductase activity</em> (GO: 0016491; 79 proteins) (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a> and <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). In the BP category, most of the proteins were involved in the <em>cellular amide biosynthetic process</em> (GO: 0043603, 90 proteins), as well as in the <em>organonitrogen compound biosynthetic process</em> (GO: 1901566; 114 proteins), and <em>oxidation–reduction process</em> (GO: 0055114, 94 proteins) (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a> and <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). The distribution of the identified proteins according to their abundance in the cellular components was associated with <em>intracellular structures</em> (GO: 0005622, 241 proteins), most of them were predicted to be localized in the <em>cytoplasm</em> (GO: 0005737, 133 proteins) and <em>ribosome</em> (GO: 0005840, 49 proteins) (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a> and <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) (detailed description in <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S3</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/b58ca93d9e6f/gr4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="220" width="719" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>

About ten top subcategories from each category are marked by a number and described in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>.
