</div></section></section><section id="sec2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">DRPs Establish a Complex Network of Interactions</h3>
<p id="p0205">The network of protein interactions was performed by submitting only DRPs (115 proteins) to Cytoscape (version 3.8.0.; NIGMS) and analyzed by stringApp (version 1.5.1.) (<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">49</a>). All interactions were shown in connection with coexpression, co-occurrence, and because of the appearance of any information on the interactions between those proteins in different databases (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figs. 5</a> and <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a>). The analysis demonstrated strong interaction networks (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figs. 5</a> and <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a>). According to the stringApp (version 1.5.1.), a total of 48 proteins constituted a very complex and strongly interactive network (275 interactions) at lower concentration of LPS (0.2 μg/ml [<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>], whereas only 21 DRPs were metabolically related [50 interactions] at higher concentration of LPS [1 μg/ml]—[<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>] (<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49">49</a>)). The remaining input proteins not present in the database or not connected with any other protein were excluded from <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figures 5</a> and <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a>. This mainly reflects the fact that those interactions have been identified on the background of <em>C. elegans</em>, a free-living species, not a parasitic one like <em>A. simplex</em> s.s, what was already discussed previously (<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36">36</a>, <a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37">37</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/8d040b39fde4/gr5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="642" width="688" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>Protein–protein interaction network analysis of differentially regulated proteins after treatment with LPS (0.2 μg/ml) in L3 larvae of <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. performed in Cytoscape, version 3.8.0.</strong> Downregulated proteins are marked in <em>red</em> and upregulated proteins in <em>green</em>. The lager the circle is, the smaller the <em>p</em> value was, which means that the protein modulation is more statistically significant. <em>Thick lines</em> indicate strong interactions. LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/3598b885b396/gr6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="584" width="647" alt="Fig. 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>Protein–protein interaction network analysis of differentially regulated proteins after treatment with LPS (1 μg/ml) in L3 larvae of <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. performed in Cytoscape, versison 3.8.0.</strong> Downregulated proteins are marked in <em>red</em> and upregulated in <em>green</em>. The larger the circle is, the smaller the <em>p</em> value was, which means that the protein modulation is more statistically significant. Thick lines indicate strong interactions. LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0210">Based on Reactome pathway analysis, the most complex nodes of the interactions were related to signal transduction (CEL-162582) and signaling by nuclear receptors (CEL-9006931) in a network constituted by DRPs after 1 μg/ml of LPS treatment (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>), whereas neutrophil degranulation (CEL-6798695), innate immune system (CEL-168249), immune system (CEL-168256), and translation (CEL-72766)—in a network constituted by DRPs after 0.2 μg/ml of LPS treatment (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>).</p></section><section id="sec2.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Oxidative Stress Markers Confirm the Impact of LPS on <em>A. simplex</em> s.s.</h3>
<p id="p0215">The activity of PRDXs in the L3 larvae of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. after stimulation by LPS was estimated by measuring the diminishment of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> level by ferrous oxidation–xylenol orange assay compared with the control, where the lower quantity of remaining H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> indicates the higher activity of PRDXs (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a><em>A</em>). The activity of PRDXs in the larvae treated with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS was calculated as 2.2 μmol/l ± 0.11 remaining H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (n = 3), whereas in the larvae treated with 1 μg/ml of LPS was calculated as 11.92 μmol/l ± 0.596 remaining H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (n = 3). The activity of PRDXs in both groups of the LPS-treated larvae was significantly higher comparing to the control (17.22 μmol/l ± 0.86 remaining H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>). Moreover, the activity of PRDXs was higher in the larvae treated with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS, than in those treated with 1 μg/ml of LPS.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/a78f1cea6bf9/gr7.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1075" width="690" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>Different biochemical indicators of oxidative stress in <em>Anisakis simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae treated with different LPS concentrations.</strong> Visualization of results of performed biochemical analyses: (<em>A</em>) peroxiredoxin activity, (<em>B</em>) total antioxidant capacity, (<em>C</em>) glutathione GSH content, and (<em>D</em>) glutathione S-transferase activity. All results were presented as mean ± SD (n = 3). Significant differences between LPS-treated samples and control samples are shown (<em>t</em> test), as well as between the treatments (<em>t</em> test). <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant, where 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗). LPS, lipopolysaccharide.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0220">The total antioxidant capacity was significantly higher in both LPS-treated groups of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. larvae compared with the control (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a><em>B</em>). This also confirms the oxidative stress induction in the presented experimental setup.</p>
<p id="p0225">The decreased content of the reduced form of GSH is an indicative of greater oxidative stress. In the larvae treated with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS, the GSH content was significantly lower compared with the control, which indicates GSH oxidation to protect cells by neutralizing ROS. In the larvae treated with 1 μg/ml of LPS, the GSH content was significantly higher compared with the control. This indicates its increased synthesis during the LPS treatment. GST activity was associated with the content of GSH, which is the substrate of this enzyme; the higher the activity of the enzyme, the lower the content of GSH. This was confirmed by a higher GST activity in the larvae treated with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <em>C</em> and <em>D</em>).</p></section><section id="sec2.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Gene Expression of Selected Markers of Oxidative Stress</h3>
<p id="p0230">The results of real-time PCR showed that the mRNA levels of two genes (<em>prdx-2</em> and <em>prdx-3</em>) were approximately consistent with the protein levels in the larvae treated with LPS (LPS 0.2 or LPS 1). The <em>prdx-2</em> gene expression as well as its protein abundance were both upregulated in LPS 0.2 samples. Moreover, downregulation of <em>prdx-3</em> expression and protein abundance was noticed in both treatments compared with the control (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>). Nevertheless, the mRNA levels of <em>prdx-1</em> and both <em>thioredoxin domain–containing proteins</em> were inconsistent with the protein abundance levels.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8605257_gr8.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/217a/8605257/6c25c31a2c25/gr8.jpg" loading="lazy" height="2082" width="652" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p><strong>The mRNA expression (<em>left</em>) and protein abundance (<em>right</em>).</strong> The protein abundance was normalized between the samples and is originally from LC–MS/MS analysis. The transcription levels of selected genes encoding chosen DRPs were obtained from real-time PCR. The control is normalized to a value 1, and the graphs indicate the changes relative to the control. <em>p</em> Values were considered statistically significant, where 0.0332 (∗), 0.0021 (∗∗), 0.0002 (∗∗∗), and &lt;0.0001 (∗∗∗∗). DRP, differentially regulated protein.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p id="p0235">The biome of the human body contains a diverse range of microorganisms named the microbiota, including bacteria, viruses, fungi, protozoa, and helminths (<a href="#bib54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib54">54</a>). The prevalence of coinfection exceeds one-sixth of the global population (<a href="#bib55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib55">55</a>). This concept is of considerable interest because microorganism interactions can affect host pathology and their own virulence evolution (<a href="#bib56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib56">56</a>). Many factors can affect or change the relationship between commensal bacteria and intestinal parasites, such as competition for food resources (<a href="#bib57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib57">57</a>), the secretion of bacterial growth inhibitors by certain species (<a href="#bib58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib58">58</a>, <a href="#bib59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib59">59</a>), and host age and diet (<a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15">15</a>). The interaction between the host, intestinal parasites, and commensal bacteria should be studied, because of the fact that parasites can cause direct or indirect changes in the bacteria, and vice versa. At the same time, the latest hypothesis is that intestinal bacteria and intestinal parasites do not have to compete in the same environment. These interactions can be neutral, harmful, or have beneficial effects (<a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15">15</a>, <a href="#bib16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib16">16</a>). This crosstalk can be considered as a specific metabolic integration system (<a href="#bib60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib60">60</a>). This hypothesis, however, has not been clearly proven and requires further research (<a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28">28</a>, <a href="#bib61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib61">61</a>).</p>
<p id="p0240">In the present study, according to our knowledge, we explored for the first time the response of parasitic nematode, <em>A. simplex</em> s.s., to the treatment with LPS isolated from <em>E. coli</em>, a Gram-negative bacterium, which is frequently the first to colonize human infants and is a lifelong colonizer of adults (<a href="#bib62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib62">62</a>). All Gram-negative bacteria are enclosed by an outer membrane, which acts as an additional protection barrier preventing the entry of toxic compounds including antibiotics and antimicrobial peptides (<a href="#bib63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib63">63</a>). The outer membrane component, LPS plays a crucial role in the antimicrobial susceptibility of <em>E. coli</em> (<a href="#bib64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib64">64</a>). LPS is one of the virulent determinants in <em>E. coli</em> that enhances its pathogenicity to provoke septicemia and resistance against antibiotics (<a href="#bib64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib64">64</a>). Moreover, LPS is classified as thymus-independent type 1 antigen, which means that it can activate B cells without T-cell help (<a href="#bib65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib65">65</a>). At the biochemical level, LPS is recognized by the Toll-like receptors preferentially expressed on phagocytes, dendritic cells, and epithelial cells at sites of bacterial entry to the host. However, it was confirmed that the existence of host responses to LPS does not require Toll-like receptor 4 (<a href="#bib65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib65">65</a>, <a href="#bib66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib66">66</a>). Thus, it can be concluded that LPS is one of the first molecules responsible for the interaction with the host or with coexisting microorganism in the host's body, for example, helminth.</p>
<p id="p0245">Therefore, to determine the response of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. during interaction with LPS, the parasite proteome profile and activity of oxidative stress mediators in the larvae were analyzed.</p>
<p id="p0250">According to literature data and our preliminary studies on the expression of <em>gdf 11</em> (homolog of human TGF-β), two doses of LPS have been selected for <em>in vitro</em> experiments. It is well known that helminths use the immunomodulatory ability of the TGF-β pathway to drive host cells to produce this cytokine and promote the long-term establishment of the parasite in mammalian tissues (<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40">40</a>, <a href="#bib67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib67">67</a>). Literature data confirmed that helminths encode endogenous members of the TGF-β ligand and receptor family, which can interact with cognate partners of vertebrate origin (<a href="#bib68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib68">68</a>, <a href="#bib69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib69">69</a>, <a href="#bib70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib70">70</a>, <a href="#bib71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib71">71</a>). Up to now, various zoonotic nematode species like <em>Ancylostoma caninum</em> and <em>Brugia malayi</em>, and trematodes such as <em>Fasciola hepatica</em>, are able to produce proteins (<a href="#bib72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib72">72</a>), which mimic host's TGF-β by replicating its biological and functional properties. This in consequence abates inflammation <em>in vivo</em> (<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40">40</a>). Therefore, in our <em>in vitro</em> model, we tested the response of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. to LPS doses determined on the expression of the <em>A. simplex gdf 11</em> previously identified by Łopieńska-Biernat <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41">41</a>) during genome-wide analysis of this species (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a><em>A</em>). The most significant changes in <em>gdf 11</em> expression after treatment with various doses of LPS prompted us to select two of them to design a proteomics experiment (0.2 and 1 μg/ml).</p>
<p id="p0255">Proteomic analysis by LS–MS/MS revealed in a total 1148 proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental File S2</a>) in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. L3 larvae treated with LPS. The results indicate that 115 proteins were differentially regulated in the LPS-treated larvae when compared with the controls (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>). These proteins formed a very diverse group (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>), including those with enzymatic, regulatory, and immunological properties, or those participating in oxidative stress response (<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p id="p0260">The present results revealed that proteins regulating the immune metabolic pathways, described previously in <em>C. elegans</em>, have not been found in the larvae L3 of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. However, we identified other proteins that can participate in the control of the immune response. For example, cathepsins act classically as lysosomal hydrolases that digest endogenous and exogenous endocytosed polypeptides (<a href="#bib73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib73">73</a>), but in parasitic nematodes, they have primary roles in larval migration, molting, immune evasion, and in cleaving intact hemoglobin before it can be processed by other digestive peptidases. In our study, we identified cathepsin D (UniProt: D0VFG1) in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. larvae after treatment with 0.2 μg/ml of LPS.</p>
<p id="p0265">In addition, in the current study, we confirmed the presence of the macrophage migration inhibitory factor (MIF; UniProt: A1Z1S6) in the proteome of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. after stimulation with LPS. MIF was one of the first cytokines to be discovered over 50 years ago (<a href="#bib74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib74">74</a>). It was confirmed that Protozoa and parasitic nematodes secrete MIF that is structurally similar to human MIF (<a href="#bib75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib75">75</a>, <a href="#bib76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib76">76</a>, <a href="#bib77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib77">77</a>). Parasitic MIF binds directly to the human MIF receptor CD74, activating the extracellular signal–regulated kinase pathway with immunomodulatory effects on variety of immune and epithelial cells. Recently, the studies on <em>A. ceylanicum</em> demonstrated that excess of parasite MIF partially displaces human MIF from its cognate receptor. It remains to be elucidated whether parasite MIF acts as an agonist, driving activation of downstream proinflammatory pathways, or as an antagonist, affecting CD74 in a nonproductive or an inhibitory fashion (<a href="#bib78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib78">78</a>). Both scenarios could result in the modulation of the host immune response. It has been reported that MIF, isolated from <em>Haemonchus contortus</em>, decreased the production of tumor necrosis factor α, interleukin 1β (IL-1β) and IL-12p40, whereas it significantly increased the secretion of IL-10 and TGF-β in goat monocytes. In the same study, MIF-1 diminished the LPS-induced nitric oxide production <em>via</em> goat monocytes and the expression of major histocompatibility complex (MHC)-II on the monocytes (<a href="#bib79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib79">79</a>). In our study, unexpectedly, the MIF protein expression was downregulated in the <em>A. simplex</em> larvae treated with both doses of LPS when compared with the control individuals (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>). This could be caused by the presence/absence of some other signals from the host organism, which would trigger the parasite to modulate the host's immune system and evade immune defenses. Moreover, our experimental setup analyzed the proteomic response of the nematodes to the bacterial LPS. This also could be a reason for the downregulation of MIF expression protein in parasitic larvae. The simulation of <em>E. coli</em> coexistence with the parasitic larvae, within the host organism, and taking over by bacteria the modulation of host immunity, might cause the downregulation of the parasite mechanisms of host's immune system modulation. However, such conclusion should be validated in further experiments.</p>
<p id="p0270">In presented cohort of the DRPs, we also found endoplasmic reticulum aminopeptidase 1 (ERAP1), which is primarily responsible for the generation of the antigenic repertoire presented by MHC-I molecules, in the control of susceptibility of different infectious diseases (<a href="#bib80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib80">80</a>). ERAP1 is mainly responsible for the shaping of peptides through the antigen processing machinery origin in the cytoplasm by the proteasome that cuts intracellular proteins into heterogeneous fragments. Peptides are subsequently transported by the transporter associated with antigen processing within the endoplasmic reticulum, where they are trimmed at the N terminus, to generate suitable length peptides to be bound by MHC-I molecules and presented on cells' surface. In humans, ERAP1 and ERAP2 are the main aminopeptidases responsible for N-terminal peptide trimming (<a href="#bib81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib81">81</a>). ERAP1, besides its significant function in acquired immunity, plays a key role in innate immune regulation. There is evidence that ERAP1 in the initial stages of pathogen recognition promotes shedding of cytokine receptors and nitric oxide formation, induces natural killer cells development and function, and triggers the phagocytic activity of macrophages (<a href="#bib80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib80">80</a>). In our study, the ERAP1 expression was downregulated in the larvae treated with both doses of LPS compared with the controls (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>), but the functions of this protein require more specific research like it was already explained in the previous example.</p>
<p id="p0275">Nematodes, like other living organisms, have evolved physiological mechanisms to respond to different pathogens by activating specific gene expression and protein production (<a href="#bib82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib82">82</a>). The current study revealed the presence of antioxidative proteins among various DRPs. There is evidence that parasite survival depends upon endogenous antioxidant defense system (<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">26</a>). To confirm the activity of this system in L3 larvae of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. after LPS treatment, different biochemical analyses were carried out (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>). Herein, we described the activity of antioxidant proteins, PRDXs, in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. The PRDXs, in prokaryotes as well as in eukaryotes, are considered to be the primary cellular guardians against oxidative stress by sensing and detoxifying H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>. Although PRDXs have been initially thought to be much less efficient peroxide reductases than catalases and GSH peroxidases, it was proven that they reduce more than 90% of cellular H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> (<a href="#bib83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib83">83</a>, <a href="#bib84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib84">84</a>). Nonetheless, their central role as peroxide scavenging enzymes among the cellular arsenal of antioxidant enzymes has been probably underestimated until recently (<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26">26</a>, <a href="#bib85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib85">85</a>, <a href="#bib86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib86">86</a>).</p>
<p id="p0280">In the group of upregulated proteins, obtained after LPS treatment, we identified PRDX-2 (F1L801), whereas PRDX-3 (A0A0M3JZW3) and two thioredoxin domain–containing proteins (A0A0B2VG46 and F1L356) were reported in the group of downregulated proteins (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">supplemental Tables S2</a> and <a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">S3</a>). Interestingly, the PRDX-1 (A0A0M3K9Z9) was found in the group of not modulated proteins. Our gene expression data on PRDXs demonstrate similar pattern—downregulation of <em>prdx-1</em> and <em>prdx-3</em>, whereas upregulation of <em>prdx-2</em>, when compared with the control (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>). Xu <em>et al.</em> (<a href="#bib87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib87">87</a>) reported a higher expression of <em>prdx-2</em> than <em>prdx-3</em> in developmental stages of <em>C. elegans</em>. It has been also reported that <em>prdx-2</em> from <em>C. elegans</em> is more important for protecting against H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> than <em>prdx-3</em> (<a href="#bib88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib88">88</a>), what is consistent with our results (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>). Similarly, <em>prx-1</em> expression level in <em>Teladorsagia circumcincta</em> was decreased in L3 development stage (<a href="#bib89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib89">89</a>). In addition, the presence of PRDX-1 in a group of not modulated proteins and its gene downregulation (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>) might directly indicate different PRDX isoform activities depending on the various stress conditions or the tissue type. For example, PRDX-1b in the adult <em>B. malayi</em> was localized in the hypodermis and lateral chord and was not secreted by/or at the surface of the larval or adult worms (<a href="#bib90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib90">90</a>). In contrast, PRDX-1a in <em>Onchocerca volvulus</em> was found in the larval and adult hypodermis and cuticle and appears to be secreted by both (<a href="#bib91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib91">91</a>, <a href="#bib92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib92">92</a>). The closely related homolog <em>Bm</em> PRDX-1a (<a href="#bib90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib90">90</a>) could be likewise surface localized or secreted, as it is antigenic in mice (<a href="#bib93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib93">93</a>) and humans (<a href="#bib94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib94">94</a>, <a href="#bib95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib95">95</a>). The PRDX in pine wood nematode, <em>Bursaphelenchus xylophilus</em>, was broadly expressed across different tissues and could be secreted outside the nematode (<a href="#bib96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib96">96</a>). In the present study, the gene expression of two <em>thioredoxin domain–containing proteins</em> (A0A0B2VG46 and F1L356) was upregulated compared with the control (<a href="#fig8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>). The expression of thioredoxins in <em>H. contortus</em> throughout the life cycle was also evaluated by quantitative real-time PCR. This study demonstrated that <em>trx-5</em> was expressed in third-stage larvae at levels like those of <em>trx-1</em> and <em>trx-3</em>, so in this case, the differentiation according to the isoform in a specific tissue has not taken place (<a href="#bib97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib97">97</a>, <a href="#bib98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib98">98</a>). It should be highlighted that the mRNA levels of both <em>thioredoxin domain–containing proteins</em> in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. were inconsistent with its protein's abundance level. In many studies, protein expression profile is inconsistent with gene expression level (<a href="#bib99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib99">99</a>, <a href="#bib100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib100">100</a>). It should be emphasized that the regulatory processes occurring after mRNA synthesis can involve post-transcriptional and translational modifications. Moreover, the regulation of protein degradation is essential in controlling steady-state protein abundance; however, the translation efficacy is a single best predictor of protein levels (<a href="#bib100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib100">100</a>). The global overview is that most mRNAs and proteins are stable unless genes need to respond quickly to the stimulus (<a href="#bib99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib99">99</a>). All these aforementioned processes take place at a very fast pace, and thus, they may cause discrepancies at the level of gene and corresponding protein expression. However, in addition to proteomic methods, analysis of <em>A. simplex</em> larval mRNA expression can provide useful information on genes associated with parasite–human microbiome interactions. Gene expression profiles <em>versus</em> abundance of their proteins could provide clues to their direct role in facilitating parasite survival and their influence on parasite–human microbiome interactions, as well as parasite–host adaptations (<a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41">41</a>, <a href="#bib101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib101">101</a>, <a href="#bib102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib102">102</a>, <a href="#bib103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib103">103</a>, <a href="#bib104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib104">104</a>).</p>
<p id="p0285">Summarizing, parasitic nematodes as anerobic organisms might use antioxidant homeostasis, and a fully functional antioxidant defense system is crucial for their survival situations. Therefore, it is highly possible that a knockout of any of these basic cell molecular components (<em>e.g.</em>, proteins regulating oxidative stress), medication, or immunization can lead to parasite death. However, those are significant cell safeguards in all organisms, and back-up mechanisms seem to exist for each kind of detoxification action. Because of this reality, focusing on one individual cell reinforcement compound may not be adequate to deliver the parasite defenseless.</p>
<p id="p0290">It is also worth mentioning that most of the endoparasites have a much-reduced set of redox proteins than their free-living and plant-parasitic relatives (<a href="#bib105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib105">105</a>). A different response to the bacterial LPS in <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. than in <em>C. elegans</em> may also indicate parasitic variability and the system of coexistence between parasite and gut bacteria.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions</h2>
<p id="p0295">The successful development and survival of parasitic nematodes depends on their effective and flexible response to stress conditions, like harsh environment, inside their hosts. Since the life cycle of parasitic nematodes takes place entirely within host tissues, it is reasonable to assume that nematode exposure to microbiome is not uncommon, especially in the case of gastrointestinal nematodes.</p>
<p id="p0300">Our findings indicate, for the first time, the complexity of the proteomic response of parasitic nematode, <em>A. simplex</em> s.s., to bacterial LPS. This experimental setup mimics the coexistence of helminth and gut bacteria in the host. The simulation of the crosstalk between parasitic nematode and bacteria showed us the complexity of the changes occurring in the parasite organism triggered by bacterial LPS <em>in vitro</em> and led us to conclude that the obtained results are hugely valuable in planning future strategies for studying helminths and can be a crucial step in the integrated systems biology approach to describe a relationship between parasite, host, and its commensal bacteria. In addition, understanding the interrelationships of microorganisms could reveal how such symbioses can shape a host organism's biology.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Data Availability</h2>
<p id="p0305">Raw files (TMT-based proteomics of <em>A. simplex</em> s.s. in response to LPS; no. MSV000087010) are publicly and freely accessible from the MassIVE Repository (<a href="http://www.massive.ucsd.edu/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">www.massive.ucsd.edu</a>).</p></section><section id="sec6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplemental data</h2>
